## GHSA-84c9-qqpq-2pq5 · obsidian-tc

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 9.9 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The application's JWT tokens, when used via the HTTP transport, did not enforce a binding between the token and a specific vault. While tokens could specify a 'vault' claim, this claim was not used to restrict access, allowing an attacker with a valid token for one vault to access or manipulate data in any other configured vault by simply specifying a different vault ID in their requests.

### Impact

An attacker could use a token issued for a specific vault to access, modify, or delete data in any other vault configured on the server, leading to unauthorized data access and potential data loss across all vaults.

### Key diff

```
type AuthOutcome =
  | { ok: true; caller: string | null; scopes: Set<string>; vault?: string }
  | { ok: false; status: 401 | 500; reason: string };

-      vaultId: opts.vaultId,
+      vaultId: authz.vault ?? opts.vaultId,
+      vaultBound: true,

+      if (ctx.vaultBound === true) {
+        const requested = (parsed.data as { vault?: unknown } | null)?.vault;
+        if (typeof requested === "string" && requested !== ctx.vaultId)
+          throw new ObsidianTcError("forbidden", "vault is not the caller's bound vault", {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a 'vault' claim to the JWT identity and a 'vaultBound' flag to the caller context. When a token contains a 'vault' claim, the HTTP transport now binds the caller to that specific vault. A new guard in the ToolRegistry rejects any tool call where the requested 'vault' argument does not match the caller's bound vault, preventing cross-vault access.

## GHSA-897f-g5gm-fmxm · wasmtime

2026-10-23 · RUST · **CRITICAL** 9.9 · Pattern: `INTEGER_OVERFLOW→BOUNDARY`

### Root cause

The Cranelift compiler used a bit-packing scheme to store `ValueDataPacked` in a `u64`, where fields like `x` and `y` were allocated 24 bits each. This narrow field encoding, combined with a special sentinel value (0xffffffff) for `u32` values that didn't fit, led to silent overflows. When a `u32` value greater than `2^24 - 2` (excluding the sentinel) was encoded, it would be truncated, leading to miscompilation.

### Impact

An attacker could craft WebAssembly modules that, when compiled by Wasmtime, would result in miscompiled code. This miscompilation could lead to incorrect program behavior, memory corruption, or potentially arbitrary code execution within the Wasmtime sandbox, breaking its security guarantees.

### Key diff

```
--- a/cranelift/codegen/src/ir/dfg.rs
+++ b/cranelift/codegen/src/ir/dfg.rs
@@ -718,7 +718,7 @@ enum ValueData {
 /// Layout:
 ///
 /// ```plain
-///        | tag:2 |  type:14        |    x:24       | y:24          |
+///        | tag:2 |  type:14        |    x:32       | y:32          |
 ///
 /// Inst       00     ty               inst output     inst index
 /// Param      01     ty               blockparam num  block index
@@ -727,76 +727,50 @@ enum ValueData {
 /// ```
 #[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Hash)]
 #[cfg_attr(feature = "enable-serde", derive(Serialize, Deserialize))]
-struct ValueDataPacked(u64);
-
-/// Encodes a value in 0..2^32 into 0..2^n, where n is less than 32
-/// (and is implied by `mask`), by translating 2^32-1 (0xffffffff)
-/// into 2^n-1 and panic'ing on 2^n..2^32-1.
-fn encode_narrow_field(x: u32, bits: u8) -> u32 {
-    let max = (1 << bits) - 1;
-    if x == 0xffff_ffff {
-        max
-    } else {
-        debug_assert!
-            x < max,
-            "{x} does not fit into {bits} bits (must be less than {max} to \
-             allow for a 0xffffffff sentinel)"
-        );
-        x
-    }
-}
-
-/// The inverse of the above `encode_narrow_field`: unpacks 2^n-1 into
-/// 2^32-1.
-fn decode_narrow_field(x: u32, bits: u8) -> u32 {
-    if x == (1 << bits) - 1 { 0xffff_ffff } else { x }
+#[repr(Rust, packed)]
+struct ValueDataPacked {
+    x: u32,
+    y: u32,
+    flags_and_type: u16,
}
 
 impl ValueDataPacked {
-    const Y_SHIFT: u8 = 0;
-    const Y_BITS: u8 = 24;
-    const X_SHIFT: u8 = Self::Y_SHIFT + Self::Y_BITS;
-    const X_BITS: u8 = 24;
-    const TYPE_SHIFT: u8 = Self::X_SHIFT + Self::X_BITS;
+    const TYPE_SHIFT: u8 = 0;
     const TYPE_BITS: u8 = 14;
     const TAG_SHIFT: u8 = Self::TYPE_SHIFT + Self::TYPE_BITS;
     const TAG_BITS: u8 = 2;
 
-    const TAG_INST: u64 = 0;
-    const TAG_PARAM: u64 = 1;
-    const TAG_ALIAS: u64 = 2;
-    const TAG_UNION: u64 = 3;
+    const TAG_INST: u16 = 0;
+    const TAG_PARAM: u16 = 1;
+    const TAG_ALIAS: u16 = 2;
+    const TAG_UNION: u16 = 3;
 
-    fn make(tag: u64, ty: Type, x: u32, y: u32) -> ValueDataPacked {
+    fn make(tag: u16, ty: Type, x: u32, y: u32) -> ValueDataPacked {
         debug_assert!(tag < (1 << Self::TAG_BITS));
         debug_assert!(ty.repr() < (1 << Self::TYPE_BITS));
 
-        let x = encode_narrow_field(x, Self::X_BITS);
-        let y = encode_narrow_field(y, Self::Y_BITS);
-
-        ValueDataPacked(
-            (tag << Self::TAG_SHIFT)
-                | ((ty.repr() as u64) << Self::TYPE_SHIFT)
-                | ((x as u64) << Self::X_SHIFT)
-                | ((y as u64) << Self::Y_SHIFT),
-        )
+        ValueDataPacked {
+            x,
+            y,
+            flags_and_type: (tag << Self::TAG_SHIFT) | (ty.repr() << Self::TYPE_SHIFT),
+        }
     }
 
     #[inline(always)]
-    fn field(self, shift: u8, bits: u8) -> u64 {
-        (self.0 >> shift) & ((1 << bits) - 1)
+    fn field(self, shift: u8, bits: u8) -> u16 {
+        (self.flags_and_type >> shift) & ((1 << bits) - 1)
     }
 
     #[inline(always)]
     fn ty(self) -> Type {
-        let ty = self.field(ValueDataPacked::TYPE_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::TYPE_BITS) as u16;
+        let ty = self.field(ValueDataPacked::TYPE_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::TYPE_BITS);
         Type::from_repr(ty)
     }
 
     #[inline(always)]
     fn set_type(&mut self, ty: Type) {
-        self.0 &= !(((1 << Self::TYPE_BITS) - 1) << Self::TYPE_SHIFT);
-        self.0 |= (ty.repr() as u64) << Self::TYPE_SHIFT;
+        self.flags_and_type &= !(((1 << Self::TYPE_BITS) - 1) << Self::TYPE_SHIFT);
+        self.flags_and_type |= ty.repr() << Self::TYPE_SHIFT;
     }
 }
 
@@ -822,35 +796,30 @@ impl From<ValueData> for ValueDataPacked {
 impl From<ValueDataPacked> for ValueData {
     fn from(data: ValueDataPacked) -> Self {
         let tag = data.field(ValueDataPacked::TAG_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::TAG_BITS);
-        let ty = u16::try_from(data.field(ValueDataPacked::TYPE_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::TYPE_BITS))
-            .expect("Mask should ensure result fits in a u16");
-        let x = u32::try_from(data.field(ValueDataPacked::X_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::X_BITS))
-            .expect("Mask should ensure result fits in a u32");
-        let y = u32::try_from(data.field(ValueDataPacked::Y_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::Y_BITS))
-            .expect("Mask should ensure result fits in a u32");
+        let ty = data.field(ValueDataPacked::TYPE_SHIFT, ValueDataPacked::TYPE_BITS);
 
         let ty = Type::from_repr(ty);
         match tag {
             ValueDataPacked::TAG_INST => ValueData::Inst {
                 ty,
-                num: u16::try_from(x).expect("Inst result num should fit in u16"),
-                inst: Inst::from_bits(decode_narrow_field(y, ValueDataPacked::Y_BITS)),
+                num: u16::try_from(data.x).expect("Inst result num should fit in u16"),
+                inst: Inst::from_bits(data.y),
             },
             ValueDataPacked::TAG_PARAM => ValueData::Param {
                 ty,
-                num: u16::try_from(x).expect("Blockparam index should fit in u16"),
-                block: Block::from_bits(decode_narrow_field(y, ValueDataPacked::Y_BITS)),
+                num: u16::try_from(data.x).expect("Blockparam index should fit in u16"),
+                block: Block::from_bits(data.y),
             },
             ValueDataPacked::TAG_ALIAS => ValueData::Alias {
                 ty,
-                original: Value::from_bits(decode_narrow_field(y, ValueDataPacked::Y_BITS)),
+                original: Value::from_bits(data.y),
             },
             ValueDataPacked::TAG_UNION => ValueData::Union {
                 ty,
-                x: Value::from_bits(decode_narrow_field(x, ValueDataPacked::X_BITS)),
-                y: Value::from_bits(decode_narrow_field(y, ValueDataPacked::Y_BITS)),
+                x: Value::from_bits(data.x),
+                y: Value::from_bits(data.y),
             },
-            _ => panic!("Invalid tag {} in ValueDataPacked 0x{:x}", tag, data.0),
+            _ => panic!("Invalid tag {tag} in ValueDataPacked"),
         }
     }
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors `ValueDataPacked` to use a `#[repr(Rust, packed)]` struct with `u32` fields for `x` and `y`, eliminating the need for bit-packing and the `encode_narrow_field`/`decode_narrow_field` functions. This ensures that `u32` values are stored without truncation, preventing silent overflows and miscompilation.

## GHSA-pqh8-4pj7-32xx · obsidian-tc

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 9.6 · Pattern: `CORS_MISCONFIGURATION→ORIGIN`

### Root cause

The obsidian-tc HTTP transport, when bound to a loopback address, lacked proper validation of the 'Host' and 'Origin' headers. This allowed a malicious web page to bypass same-origin policy restrictions via DNS rebinding attacks or by directly crafting requests to the loopback address, tricking the browser into thinking it was communicating with a legitimate server.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious web page that, when visited by a user, could make requests to the local obsidian-tc server running on the user's machine. This could lead to unauthorized access to the user's vault data or execution of arbitrary actions within the application, effectively enabling a drive-by attack.

### Key diff

```
- import { normalizeHostForBind, type ServerConfig } from "@the-40-thieves/obsidian-tc-shared";
+ import {
+   isLoopbackHost,
+   normalizeHostForBind,
+   type ServerConfig,
+ } from "@the-40-thieves/obsidian-tc-shared";
@@ -77,6 +87,37 @@ export function createHttpApp(opts: HttpAppOptions): Hono {
       : null);
 
   app.post("/mcp", async (c) => {
+    if (opts.enableDnsRebindingProtection !== false) {
+      const rawHost = c.req.header("host") ?? "";
+      const hostname = rawHost.replace(/:\d+$/, "").replace(/^[\[|\]]$/g, "");
+      const hostAllowed =
+        isLoopbackHost(hostname) ||
+        hostname === "localhost" ||
+        (opts.allowedHosts ?? []).includes(rawHost) ||
+        (opts.allowedHosts ?? []).includes(hostname);
+      if (!hostAllowed)
+        return c.json(
+          { jsonrpc: "2.0", error: { code: -32000, message: "host not allowed" }, id: null },
+          403,
+        );
+      const origin = c.req.header("origin");
+      if (origin) {
+        const allowed = new Set(opts.allowedOrigins ?? []);
+        allowed.add(`http://${rawHost}`);
+        allowed.add(`https://${rawHost}`);
+        if (!allowed.has(origin))
+          return c.json(
+            { jsonrpc: "2.0", error: { code: -32000, message: "origin not allowed" }, id: null },
+            403,
+          );
+      }
+    }
     const authz = await resolveAuth(c.req.header("authorization"), opts.auth, verifier);
     if (!authz.ok) {
       return c.json(
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces 'Host' and 'Origin' header validation for incoming HTTP requests. By default, it rejects requests where the 'Host' header is not a loopback address or explicitly allowed, and where the 'Origin' header is not the same origin or explicitly allowed. This prevents DNS rebinding and cross-origin attacks against the local server.

## GHSA-53xc-49m8-p798 · Twisted

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The Twisted IRC server's `IRC` protocol implementation did not properly limit the size of incoming lines. An attacker could send a continuous stream of data without a line delimiter, causing the server's internal buffer (`self.buffer`) to grow indefinitely, leading to memory exhaustion.

### Impact

An unauthenticated remote attacker could cause the IRC server to consume all available memory, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users and potentially crashing the server process.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/twisted/words/protocols/irc.py
+++ b/src/twisted/words/protocols/irc.py
@@ -421,12 +424,14 @@ def dataReceived(self, data):
         if len(self.buffer) > self.MAX_LENGTH:
             # An unterminated line too long to be a valid message.
             line, self.buffer = self.buffer, ""
-            return self.lineLengthExceeded(line)
+            self.lineLengthExceeded(line)
+            return
 
         for line in lines:
             if len(line) > self.MAX_LENGTH:
                 self.buffer = ""
-                return self.lineLengthExceeded(line)
+                self.lineLengthExceeded(line)
+                return
             if len(line) <= 2:
                 # This is a blank line, at best.
                 continue
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `MAX_LENGTH` constant (16384 bytes) to the `IRC` class. The `dataReceived` method now checks if the incoming line or the accumulated buffer exceeds this limit. If the limit is exceeded, the connection is immediately dropped by calling `self.transport.loseConnection()`, preventing unbounded buffer growth.

## GHSA-f2hq-32vh-53cc · postal-mime

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from inefficient handling of RFC 2047 base64 encoded words in email headers. Specifically, the `canJoinWords` function repeatedly checked `left.encodedText.length % 4 === 0 && !/=$/.test(left.encodedText)` on a string that grew quadratically in length with each concatenation. This regex test on a progressively longer string led to quadratic CPU consumption.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious email header containing many short, joinable RFC 2047 base64 encoded words. Processing such a header would consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service for the application or server processing the email.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/decode-strings.ts
+++ b/src/decode-strings.ts
@@ -611,7 +616,11 @@ function canJoinWords(left: EncodedWordToken, right: EncodedWordToken): boolean
     }
 
     if (encoding === 'B') {
-        return left.encodedText.length % 4 === 0 && !/=$/.test(left.encodedText);
+        // The length of a string built by concatenation is stored with it, but a regex or
+        // endsWith on it flattens everything joined so far first. Testing the growing text
+        // for a pad on every word made a subject of a hundred thousand short words cost
+        // seconds, so the pad is tracked as the words are appended instead.
+        return left.encodedText.length % 4 === 0 && !left.padded;
     }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `PendingWord` interface that tracks whether the last appended encoded word was padded (`padded: boolean`). Instead of re-evaluating the regex `!/=$/.test(left.encodedText)` on the entire concatenated string, the `canJoinWords` function now simply checks the `padded` flag. This avoids the quadratic complexity by performing a constant-time check.

## GHSA-gx7p-2j49-hfq4 · vllm

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vLLM server allowed unvalidated `mm_processor_kwargs` and `media_io_kwargs` to be passed in API requests. These parameters could be used to control resource-intensive multimodal preprocessing and media loading operations, leading to potential resource exhaustion.

### Impact

An attacker could send specially crafted requests with malicious `mm_processor_kwargs` or `media_io_kwargs` to consume excessive CPU, memory, or I/O resources, causing the vLLM server to become unresponsive or crash, thereby achieving a Denial of Service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/vllm/entrypoints/generate/base/protocol.py
+++ b/vllm/entrypoints/generate/base/protocol.py
@@ -49,6 +49,34 @@ def validate_cache_salt(cache_salt: object) -> None:
         )
 
 
+def validate_request_mm_kwargs(
+    *, 
+    mm_processor_kwargs: dict[str, Any] | None,
+    media_io_kwargs: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] | None,
+    trust_request_mm_kwargs: bool,
+) -> None:
+    """Reject untrusted per-request multimodal kwarg overrides."""
+    if trust_request_mm_kwargs:
+        return
+
+    if mm_processor_kwargs:
+        raise VLLMValidationError(
+            "Per-request mm_processor_kwargs are disabled by default because "
+            "they can change multimodal preprocessing resource usage. Start "
+            "the server with --trust-request-mm-kwargs only when clients "
+            "are trusted.",
+            parameter="mm_processor_kwargs",
+        )
+    if media_io_kwargs:
+        raise VLLMValidationError(
+            "Per-request media_io_kwargs are disabled by default because "
+            "they can change multimodal media loading resource usage. Start "
+            "the server with --trust-request-mm-kwargs only when clients "
+            "are trusted.",
+            parameter="media_io_kwargs",
+        )
+
+
 class SpeculativeDecodingMetrics(OpenAIBaseModel):
     """Per-request speculative-decoding acceptance metrics.
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a validation mechanism for `mm_processor_kwargs` and `media_io_kwargs`. By default, these per-request parameters are now rejected unless the server is explicitly started with `--trust-request-mm-kwargs`. This prevents untrusted clients from manipulating resource usage.

## GHSA-q7qp-rjg7-q26m · multer

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because Multer's `fileFilter` could remain pending asynchronously even after the HTTP request had been torn down. If a subsequent error occurred on the `busboy` stream after the request was finished, and no error listener was present, it could lead to an unhandled exception and crash the Node.js process.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger a Denial of Service by causing the Node.js application to crash, making the service unavailable to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/make-middleware.js
+++ b/lib/make-middleware.js
@@ -160,6 +160,9 @@ function makeMiddleware (setup) {
         req.unpipe(busboy)
         setImmediate(() => {
           busboy.removeAllListeners()
+          // Keep an error listener so a late busboy error after teardown cannot
+          // crash the process; the request has already finished.
+          busboy.on('error', function () {})
         })
       }
       drainStream(req)
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds an empty error listener to the `busboy` stream after the request has been unpiped and listeners removed. This ensures that any late errors on the `busboy` stream, occurring after the request teardown, are caught and do not crash the process.

## GHSA-xcx6-jx8p-x3h5 · @azure/msal-node

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `INFO_DISCLOSURE→ERROR_MESSAGE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability arises because the `acquireTokenByCode` method in `ConfidentialClientApplication` did not properly sanitize the `authCodePayload` parameter. When `instanceAware` configuration was enabled, it could lead to sensitive parameters being included in the authorization code flow, potentially disclosing them to an attacker-controlled endpoint if the redirect URI was manipulated.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially intercept or observe sensitive parameters (like `nonce` or `state`) from the authorization code flow, which could be used in further attacks like session hijacking or bypassing certain security checks, especially if the redirect URI was under attacker control.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/msal-node/src/client/ConfidentialClientApplication.ts
+++ b/lib/msal-node/src/client/ConfidentialClientApplication.ts
@@ -69,6 +73,13 @@ export class ConfidentialClientApplication
      * @param Configuration - configuration object for the MSAL ConfidentialClientApplication instance
      */
     constructor(configuration: Configuration) {
+        if (
+            "instanceAware" in configuration.auth &&
+            configuration.auth.instanceAware === true
+        ) {
+            throw NodeAuthError.createUnsupportedCcaInstanceAwareError();
+        }
+
         super(configuration);
 
         const clientSecretNotEmpty = !!this.config.auth.clientSecret;
@@ -132,6 +143,45 @@ export class ConfidentialClientApplication
         this.appTokenProvider = undefined;
     }
 
+    /**
+     * Creates the URL of the authorization request.
+     */
+    public async getAuthCodeUrl(
+        request: AuthorizationUrlRequest
+    ): Promise<string> {
+        if (
+            request.extraQueryParameters &&
+            Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(
+                request.extraQueryParameters,
+                "instance_aware"
+            )
+        ) {
+            throw NodeAuthError.createUnsupportedCcaInstanceAwareError(
+                request.correlationId
+            );
+        }
+
+        return super.getAuthCodeUrl(request);
+    }
+
+    /**
+     * Acquires a token by exchanging an authorization code.
+     */
+    public async acquireTokenByCode(
+        request: AuthorizationCodeRequest,
+        authCodePayload?: AuthorizationCodePayload
+    ): Promise<AuthenticationResult> {
+        const sanitizedAuthCodePayload = authCodePayload
+            ? {
+                  code: request.code,
+                  state: authCodePayload.state,
+                  nonce: request.nonce,
+              }
+            : undefined;
+
+        return super.acquireTokenByCode(request, sanitizedAuthCodePayload);
+    }
+
     /**
      * This extensibility point only works for the client_credential flow, i.e. acquireTokenByClientCredential and
      * is meant for Azure SDK to enhance Managed Identity support.
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces checks to prevent `ConfidentialClientApplication` from being used in an `instanceAware` configuration, throwing an error if attempted. It also explicitly sanitizes the `authCodePayload` in `acquireTokenByCode` to only include `code`, `state`, and `nonce`, preventing other potentially sensitive parameters from being passed through.

## GHSA-xjj6-f33q-929c · undici

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The `undici` library did not properly handle responses with an excessive number of `content-encoding` headers. When such a response was encountered, the error was rejected but not properly aborted, leading to an unhandled error that could crash the application.

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted HTTP response with many `content-encoding` headers, causing the `undici` client to crash and leading to a denial of service for applications using the library.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/web/fetch/index.js
+++ b/lib/web/fetch/index.js
@@ -2234,7 +2232,7 @@ async function httpNetworkFetch (
             // CVE fix similar to urllib3 (GHSA-gm62-xv2j-4w53) and curl (CVE-2022-32206).
             const maxContentEncodings = 5
             if (codings.length > maxContentEncodings) {
-              reject(new Error(`too many content-encodings in response: ${codings.length}, maximum allowed is ${maxContentEncodings}`))
+              this.abort(new Error(`too many content-encodings in response: ${codings.length}, maximum allowed is ${maxContentEncodings}`))
               return true
             }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a limit on the number of `content-encoding` headers (maximum 5). If this limit is exceeded, the `this.abort()` method is now called instead of `reject()`, ensuring the error is properly handled and the connection is terminated, preventing an application crash.

## GHSA-jr5w-w6x2-84p9 · electron

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

Node.js's TLS implementation lacked an equivalent to Chromium's Subject Public Key Info (SPKI) blocklist. This allowed certificate chains containing public keys that were known to be compromised (e.g., from the eMudhra incident) to still be validated as long as they chained up to a trusted root certificate authority.

### Impact

An attacker could perform man-in-the-middle attacks by presenting a certificate issued by a compromised intermediate certificate authority, which would be trusted by applications using Electron/Node.js, leading to interception and manipulation of encrypted communications.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/crypto/crypto_context.cc
+++ b/src/crypto/crypto_context.cc
@@ -1562,6 +1665,7 @@ void SecureContext::Init(const FunctionCallbackInfo<Value>& args) {
 
   env->external_memory_accounter()->Increase(env->isolate(), kExternalSize);
   SSL_CTX_set_app_data(sc->ctx_.get(), sc);
++  SSL_CTX_set_cert_verify_callback(sc->ctx_.get(), CertVerifyCallback, nullptr);
 
   // OpenSSL populates cert_comp_prefs with all available algorithms by
   // default when compression libraries are linked. Clear them so that
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a SPKI blocklist into Node.js's TLS verification process. It wraps the X509_verify_cert function to check if any certificate in the chain (or the peer's certificate if no chain is built) has a public key hash matching an entry in a compiled-in blocklist. If a match is found, the certificate is rejected with X509_V_ERR_CERT_REVOKED.

## GHSA-v4x8-3wr5-3wq8 · filelock

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The `filelock` library used SQLite for its ReadWriteLock implementation. When SQLite reopens a database by name, it does not re-validate the inode. An attacker could plant a symlink or a journal file at the expected lock file path, causing the legitimate process to interact with an arbitrary file on the filesystem instead of the intended lock file.

### Impact

An attacker could cause the `filelock` user's process to overwrite or unlink arbitrary files on the system, leading to data corruption, denial of service, or potentially other impacts depending on the affected files.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/filelock/_read_write.py
+++ b/src/filelock/_read_write.py
@@ -78,8 +85,7 @@
 _MAX_TRANSITION_WAIT: Final[float] = 0.05
 # O_NONBLOCK keeps an open from blocking on a FIFO planted at the path; the regular-file check then rejects it.
 _DB_OPEN_FLAGS: Final[int] = os.O_RDWR | os.O_CREAT | getattr(os, "O_NOFOLLOW", 0) | getattr(os, "O_NONBLOCK", 0)
-# Descriptor paths reopen the validated inode; journal_mode=MEMORY avoids an on-disk journal at the synthetic path.
-_FD_DIR: Final[str] = "/proc/self/fd" if sys.platform == "linux" else "/dev/fd"
+_DIRECTORY_FLAGS: Final[int] = os.O_RDONLY | getattr(os, "O_DIRECTORY", 0) | getattr(os, "O_NOFOLLOW", 0)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a mechanism to create a private, hard-linked directory for the SQLite lock file. This ensures that SQLite always operates on a file within a controlled, isolated directory, preventing symlink attacks. It also removes the use of `/proc/self/fd` or `/dev/fd` for opening database files, which was susceptible to this issue.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 74 occurrences**

## GHSA-394h-qwmc-48pf · virtualenv

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The `xonsh` activation script used `repr()` to quote strings, which relies on the script's decoding codec. If the script was read with a different encoding than expected, especially one that doesn't support ASCII, it could lead to incorrect interpretation of string literals.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious string that, when processed by `repr()` and then interpreted by `xonsh` with a mismatched encoding, could lead to arbitrary code execution within the user's shell environment.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/virtualenv/activation/xonsh/__init__.py
+++ b/src/virtualenv/activation/xonsh/__init__.py
-        return repr(string)
+        return ascii(string)
```

### Fix summary

The patch changes the string quoting mechanism from `repr()` to `ascii()`. This ensures that string literals are represented using only ASCII characters, making them independent of the script's decoding codec and preventing misinterpretation.

## GHSA-5x8w-8jj5-q52h · pillow

2026-10-23 · Python · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `BUFFER_OVERFLOW→HEAP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the scanline buffer used for JPEG decoding in FlashPix (FPX) images was sized based on tile extents and the unpacker, not on the actual JPEG header's output dimensions. This discrepancy allowed libjpeg to write more data than the allocated buffer could hold if the image's reported output width or component size exceeded the buffer's capacity.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious FlashPix image that, when processed, would lead to a heap buffer overflow. This could result in denial of service (application crash) or potentially arbitrary code execution.

### Key diff

```
if (context->cinfo.output_width > (JDIMENSION)state->xsize ||
                (size_t)context->cinfo.output_components * context->cinfo.output_width >
                    (size_t)state->bytes) {
                jpeg_destroy_decompress(&context->cinfo);
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check to ensure that the `output_width` and the total size required by `output_components * output_width` from the JPEG context do not exceed the allocated `state->xsize` and `state->bytes` respectively. If an overflow condition is detected, the JPEG decompressor is destroyed, and an error code is returned, preventing the buffer overflow.

## GHSA-6c7f-9265-g8fw · pypdf

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from inefficient handling of missing indirect objects. When an indirect object was referenced multiple times but not found, the system repeatedly performed expensive full-stream searches (reading the entire PDF stream) instead of caching the 'not found' status, leading to excessive CPU and I/O usage.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious PDF file that, when processed by pypdf, would cause the application to consume excessive CPU and memory resources, potentially leading to a denial of service for legitimate users or processes.

### Key diff

```
--- a/pypdf/_reader.py
+++ b/pypdf/_reader.py
@@ -544,11 +545,17 @@ def get_object(
         retval = self.cache_get_indirect_object(
             indirect_reference.generation, indirect_reference.idnum
         )
-        if retval is not None:
+        key = (
+            indirect_reference.generation,
+            indirect_reference.idnum,
+        )
+        if key in self.resolved_objects:
             return retval
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `resolved_objects` cache to store the resolution status of indirect objects, including those that are not found. It also refactors the stream reading logic into a new `get_stream_data` utility function to centralize and optimize stream access, avoiding redundant full-stream reads.

## GHSA-737x-cwfr-5vvc · obsidian-tc

2026-10-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The application displayed untrusted user-supplied text (like file paths) directly in confirmation forms without sufficient sanitization. This allowed special Unicode characters and backticks to manipulate the rendered output, creating fake lines or reordering text.

### Impact

An attacker could craft malicious input that, when displayed in a human-in-the-loop confirmation prompt, could mislead a user into approving a destructive action (e.g., deletion, overwrite) by spoofing the confirmation message or adding deceptive reassurances.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/server/src/mcp/elicit-form.ts
+++ b/packages/server/src/mcp/elicit-form.ts
@@ -107,7 +107,7 @@
  * NOT strip (letters, digits, ordinary punctuation) — the claim is "no character can forge the
  * QUOTING," not "no character needs stripping first."
  */
-export function sanitizeDisplayText(value: string, maxLen = 200): string {
-  const stripped = value.replace(/[`]/gu, "");
+export function sanitizeDisplayText(value: string, maxLen = 200): string {
+  const stripped = value.replace(/[\p{Cc}\p{Cf}\p{Zl}\p{Zp}`]/gu, "");
   return stripped.length > maxLen ? `${stripped.slice(0, maxLen)}…` : stripped;
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `sanitizeDisplayText` function that strips all Unicode control, format, line, and paragraph separator characters, as well as backticks, from user-supplied display text. It also truncates the text to a maximum length to prevent excessively long values from crowding out legitimate instructions.

---

## GHSA-f9x3-7vq9-cph6 · laravel/framework

2026-10-23 · PHP · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `TYPE_CONFUSION→BYPASS`

### Root cause

The Laravel TokenGuard component did not explicitly validate that the API token or credential input was a string. This allowed an attacker to provide a non-string value (e.g., an array or object), which would bypass the authentication logic because the comparison or hashing operations would not be performed as expected, leading to an authenticated state without a valid token.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass authentication and gain unauthorized access to API endpoints that are protected by the TokenGuard, potentially leading to data compromise or unauthorized actions depending on the scope of the protected resources.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Illuminate/Auth/TokenGuard.php
+++ b/src/Illuminate/Auth/TokenGuard.php
@@ -80,7 +80,7 @@ public function user()
 
         $token = $this->getTokenForRequest();
 
-        if (! empty($token)) {
+        if (is_string($token) && ! empty($token)) {
             $user = $this->provider->retrieveByCredentials([
                 $this->storageKey => $this->hash ? hash('sha256', $token) : $token,
             ]);
@@ -110,7 +110,7 @@ public function getTokenForRequest()
      */
     public function validate(array $credentials = [])
     {
-        if (empty($credentials[$this->inputKey])) {
+        if (! is_string($credentials[$this->inputKey] ?? null) || empty($credentials[$this->inputKey])) {
             return false;
         }
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds explicit type checking to ensure that the API token retrieved from the request and the credentials provided for validation are strictly strings. If a non-string value is encountered, the authentication process will now correctly fail, preventing the bypass.

## GHSA-gmh7-3g9r-mjh5 · pypdf

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The pypdf library did not properly validate the 'size' field in the cross-reference (xref) table header of a PDF document. An attacker could craft a PDF with an extremely large 'size' value, leading to an excessive number of iterations in a loop when processing the xref table.

### Impact

An attacker could provide a specially crafted PDF file that, when processed by pypdf, would cause the application to enter a long-running or infinite loop, consuming excessive CPU resources and leading to a denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/pypdf/_reader.py
+++ b/pypdf/_reader.py
@@ -1065,14 +1082,8 @@ def _read_standard_xref_table(self, stream: StreamType) -> None:
             first_time = False
             read_non_whitespace(stream)
             stream.seek(-1, 1)
-            size = cast(int, read_object(stream, self))
-            if not isinstance(size, int):
-                logger_warning(
-                    "Invalid/Truncated xref table. Rebuilding it.",
-                    source=__name__,
-                )
-                self._rebuild_xref_table(stream)
-                stream.read()
+            size = self._read_standard_xref_table__handle_size(stream)
+            if size is None:
                 return
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function `_read_standard_xref_table__handle_size` that validates the 'size' field against a calculated `size_limit`. This limit is derived from the stream's total size divided by the expected entry size (20 bytes). If the declared 'size' exceeds this limit, a `LimitReachedError` is raised, preventing the excessive loop.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 31 occurrences**

## GHSA-jr96-gvgh-j3r4 · pypdf

2026-10-23 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from inefficient handling of malformed cross-reference entries in PDF files. Specifically, when `get_object` or `_read_standard_xref_table` encountered an error during parsing, it would repeatedly read the entire stream data to search for object definitions using regular expressions. This full stream read was performed multiple times for each malformed entry.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a PDF file with numerous malformed cross-reference entries, leading to excessive CPU and memory consumption due to repeated full file reads and regex searches. This could result in a denial of service for applications processing such malicious PDF files.

### Key diff

```
--- a/pypdf/_reader.py
+++ b/pypdf/_reader.py
@@ -572,13 +579,7 @@ def get_object(
                 ): 
                     raise PdfReadError("Not matching, we parse the file for it")
             except Exception:
-                if hasattr(self.stream, "getbuffer"):
-                    buf = bytes(self.stream.getbuffer())
-                else:
-                    p = self.stream.tell()
-                    self.stream.seek(0, 0)
-                    buf = self.stream.read(-1)
-                    self.stream.seek(p, 0)
+                buf = get_stream_data(self.stream)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new utility function `get_stream_data` to centralize the logic for reading the entire stream data. More importantly, it modifies the `get_object` and `_read_standard_xref_table` methods to use this utility, ensuring that the stream data is read only once and cached (`recovery_cache`) when performing recovery searches, rather than repeatedly reading the entire file.
